22.10 Evaluation, operations, security

MeasureHowTarget to track (set after the first benchmark)
recall@10Brute-force ground truth on a 10k sample of queries; fraction of true top-10 returnedAbove 0.95 at the chosen L
Latencyp50, p99 at fixed concurrency, cold and warm cachep99 in the low tens of milliseconds for a single segment on local SSD
ThroughputQueries per second per core at that recallReported, plotted against L
Build time, RAM, diskPer million vectors for R, L_build, PQ settingsReported

Benchmark data: SIFT1M (128 dimensions, L2) and GIST1M (960 dimensions, L2) for the engine; a generated corpus of Flowforge entities for end-to-end quality. Plot the recall versus QPS curve for several values of L; that single chart is the main learning artefact.

  • Real deletion: tombstoning hides a vector immediately; the physical removal happens at merge. A tenant erasure request deletes the rows, tombstones the vectors and forces a merge; the SLA is stated in terms of that merge deadline.
  • Model upgrades: a new model_version creates a second index; search keeps using the old one until the backfill reaches 100%, then flips.
  • Prompt injection and untrusted content: embedded text comes from the internet. It is data to the embedding model, never an instruction, and similarity results are not trusted as scope evidence.
  • Cost: embedding calls are metered through the same budget as other llm effects (section 16.5).

23. Distributed systems design

Flowforge is distributed from day one: several instances of each service, workers that crash, Pub/Sub that redelivers. This section states the model and the rules so that every component makes the same assumptions. It also lays out an optional path from "Postgres is the source of truth" to a self-built replicated log, for learning purposes.

23.1 Failure model and assumptions

  • Crash-stop with restart: processes may die at any instruction and later restart with durable state intact. Byzantine behaviour is out of scope.
  • Network: messages can be delayed, duplicated, dropped or reordered; partitions occur and heal. Timeouts are the only failure detector, so they are unreliable by nature.
  • Clocks: wall clocks skew and jump. Never use a wall clock to decide who owns something; use leases checked against a monotonic clock plus fencing tokens validated by the storage layer. Wall time is for display and for staleness heuristics only.
  • Delivery: Pub/Sub, Cloud Tasks and gRPC retries are at-least-once. Every handler is idempotent.
  • Principle: hashing and heartbeats are for efficiency (spread load, prefer a likely owner); leases with fencing are for safety (at most one effective writer). If hashing is wrong, work is merely duplicated or slow; if fencing is wrong, data is corrupt.

23.2 Guarantees by component

ComponentConsistencyUnder partitionMechanism
Run event logLinearizable per run (single writer, ordered seq)Choose consistency: a minority side cannot appendPostgres transaction, expected_seq plus fencing token
Run ownershipAt most one effective owner per runOld owner is fenced when it next writesLease row, fence increments on every acquisition
Effect executionAt-least-once execution, exactly-once recordingMay run twice; result recorded onceIdempotency key sha256(run_id/path), unique constraint on the result
Graph writesEventually consistent merge, convergentBoth sides accept writes, merge on healCommutative, associative, idempotent upserts (23.5)
Rate limitsApproximate, bounded errorChoose availability: local budgets continue (section 14.4)Leased budgets, CRDT counters
Scope decisionsFail closedDeny when the scope store is unreachable and no fresh cache entry existsVersioned scope documents, cache with short TTL
AlertsAt-least-once, de-duplicatedDelayed, never lostTransactional outbox, inbox keyed by event id
Similarity indexEventually consistent, watermark reportedServes stale resultsIdempotent upserts ordered by source_seq

In PACELC terms: the control plane (runs, approvals, scope) is PC/EC; the data plane (graph, search, rate limits) is PA/EL.

23.3 Work distribution

  • Membership: workers write a heartbeat row (or Redis key with TTL) every 5 seconds; a worker is considered live for 15 seconds after its last heartbeat. A gossip protocol (SWIM-style, with suspicion) is the stretch goal.
  • Rendezvous (highest-random-weight) hashing: for a key k and live workers w, the owner is argmax over w of hash(k, w). It needs no ring state and moves only the keys of a failed worker. Used for: run affinity (keep a run's VM snapshot hot), target affinity (a host's requests go through the same tool-runner, which makes per-target rate limits local and accurate).
  • Consistent hashing with virtual nodes is the alternative when the key space needs weighting; keep both implementations behind a Placement trait and test them for balance and minimal movement.
  • Claiming: the preferred owner tries to acquire the lease first; other workers delay their attempts by their rank in the hash order. If the preferred owner is dead, the next in line takes over after the lease expires. Ranks only change who tries first, never who may write.
  • Back-pressure: worker concurrency is bounded by a semaphore per tenant and per tool class; the queue is the buffer; a full queue returns RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED with retry_after.
rendezvous_owner(key, workers):  // deterministic; every node computes the same answer from the same membership
  best = none
  for w in workers:
    score = hash64(key || w.id)          // for example xxh3 or siphash with a fixed key
    if best is none or score > best.score or (score == best.score and w.id < best.id): best = (w, score)
  return best.w

Test: 1000 keys over 5 workers, remove one worker: only keys owned by the removed worker move, roughly 200,
      and the remaining distribution stays within a few percent of uniform.

23.4 The EventStore abstraction

The engine depends on a narrow trait, so the implementation can change from Postgres to a replicated log without touching the VM or the engine logic.

// Specification-level signature (Rust shown; port the semantics to any language)
pub struct Fence(pub u64, pub u64);            // (term, counter); compared lexicographically

pub trait EventStore: Send + Sync {
    /// Append events if the stream head equals `expected_seq` AND `fence` is the current fence.
    /// Atomic. Returns the new head. Errors: SeqConflict, Fenced.
    async fn append(&self, run: RunId, expected_seq: u64, fence: Fence, events: &[Event])
        -> Result<u64, AppendError>;

    async fn read(&self, run: RunId, from_seq: u64, limit: usize) -> Result<Vec<Event>, StoreError>;
    async fn subscribe(&self, run: RunId, from_seq: u64) -> Result<EventStream, StoreError>;

    async fn save_snapshot(&self, run: RunId, at_seq: u64, bytes: Bytes) -> Result<(), StoreError>;
    async fn load_snapshot(&self, run: RunId) -> Result<Option<(u64, Bytes)>, StoreError>;

    /// Lease: returns a fence strictly greater than any previous fence for this run.
    async fn acquire_lease(&self, run: RunId, owner: WorkerId, ttl: Duration) -> Result<Lease, LeaseError>;
    async fn renew_lease(&self, lease: &Lease, ttl: Duration) -> Result<Lease, LeaseError>;
    async fn release_lease(&self, lease: Lease) -> Result<(), StoreError>;

    /// Runs that are ready for work: unleased or lease expired, with a due timer or pending event.
    async fn list_ready(&self, shard: Shard, limit: usize) -> Result<Vec<RunId>, StoreError>;
}

Properties every implementation must satisfy (these become the conformance suite in 23.8): (1) after a successful append at head h the next append must name expected_seq = h + n; (2) an append carrying an older fence fails even if expected_seq matches; (3) a read after a successful append observes it (read-your-writes) and reads are never reordered; (4) acquire_lease returns strictly increasing fences, including across restarts of the store.

Implementation A: Postgres (default)

The schema in section 10.4. append is a single transaction: SELECT ... FOR UPDATE on the run row, check head_seq = expected_seq AND fence = $fence, insert events, update head_seq. Lease acquisition does UPDATE runs SET fence = fence + 1, lease_owner = $w, lease_expires = now() + $ttl WHERE run_id = $r AND (lease_expires < now() OR lease_owner = $w) RETURNING fence. The now() here is the database clock, which is the single time source for lease expiry, so worker clock skew cannot break it. list_ready uses FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED.

Implementation B: sharded Raft log (optional, learning track)

  • Sharding: shard = hash(run_id) mod S (S fixed at deployment, for example 64; resharding is done by splitting a shard's key range, not by changing S). Each shard is a Raft group of 3 or 5 replicas; the replicas of many shards share processes (multi-Raft) and share a transport.
  • State machine: apply committed commands Append{run, expected_seq, fence, events}, AcquireLease, RenewLease, SaveSnapshot. The state machine is deterministic and checks expected_seq and fence at apply time, so a stale command from a deposed leader is rejected when it commits.
  • Fence: (raft term, per-term counter). A new lease acquisition after a leader change always yields a larger term, which preserves strict monotonicity without a clock.
  • Reads: ReadIndex (leader confirms leadership with a heartbeat round, waits for the applied index to reach the commit index at request time) for linearizable reads; follower reads allowed for subscribe with a lag bound.
  • Log and snapshots: segmented write-ahead log with CRC per record and fsync policy (group commit); snapshot of the state machine every N applied entries, then truncate; install-snapshot RPC for slow followers.
  • Membership change: single-server changes (one at a time) first; joint consensus as a stretch goal.
  • Raft invariants to test: election safety (at most one leader per term), leader append-only, log matching, leader completeness, state machine safety.
  • Deployment: replicas are stateful with persistent disks, so they need GKE StatefulSets (not Cloud Run). This is the main infrastructure reason Implementation B is optional.

23.5 Convergent graph merge

Several tool-runners and several pipelines write observations for the same entity concurrently. The merge function (section 5.7) is designed so the final state does not depend on arrival order or duplication:

FieldMerge ruleCommutative / associative / idempotent
created_atminyes / yes / yes
last_seenmaxyes / yes / yes
confidenceNoisy-or over distinct sources: 1 - prod(1 - c_i), with each source contributing at most once (keep the maximum per source)yes / yes / yes (per-source max makes duplicates harmless)
tags and sourcesSet unionyes / yes / yes
scalar attributesLast-writer-wins by (observed_at, source_id) total orderyes / yes / yes
edgesKeyed by (from, label, to); upsert applies the rules aboveyes / yes / yes
deletesNever delete on observation; staleness (section 5.7) hides entities; explicit deletes are tombstones with a timestamp and win over older writes onlyyes

Property tests: for random multisets of observations, applying them in any permutation, with arbitrary duplication, gives byte-identical graph state. This is the property that lets graph ingestion run at-least-once without coordination. In database terms it is an upsert INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE whose update expressions are exactly the table above.

23.6 Distributed queries and analytics

  • Search and similarity: scatter-gather. The coordinator sends the query to every shard or segment, each returns its local top-k with distances, the coordinator merges with a k-way merge (heap), applies tombstones and filters, and returns. Hedged requests (send a duplicate to a second replica after the p95 latency) cut tail latency; deadlines propagate in gRPC metadata.
  • Graph analytics at scale (stretch): partition the CSR by node range, run Pregel / bulk-synchronous-parallel supersteps (each worker processes its vertices, sends messages to neighbours, barrier, repeat). PageRank, connected components (label propagation) and BFS map directly onto it. Start single-process with a thread-per-partition runner and message queues, then move to processes. One snapshot per program fits in one machine for the foreseeable future, so this is for learning, not need.

23.7 Messaging and cross-service consistency

  • Outbox: a service writes its state change and an outbox row in one transaction; a relay publishes to Pub/Sub and marks the row. A crash between publish and mark causes a duplicate, never a loss.
  • Inbox: consumers record the event id in an inbox table inside the transaction that applies the effect; a duplicate hits the unique constraint and is acknowledged without effect.
  • Ordering: Pub/Sub ordering keys per run or per entity where order matters; consumers still tolerate gaps by checking seq.
  • No distributed transactions: cross-service workflows are sagas with compensation (section 9.6). Two-phase commit is explicitly not used.
  • Dead letters: after N failed deliveries a message goes to a dead-letter topic with the failure reason; an operator tool replays or discards it.
  • Distributed tracing: W3C traceparent in gRPC metadata and in Pub/Sub attributes, so one trace spans gateway, engine, tool-runner, graph and notifier.

23.8 Failure scenarios and how they are tested

ScenarioExpected behaviourInvariant checked
Worker killed mid-runLease expires, another worker acquires a higher fence, replays the log, continuesRun completes; event log has no gaps or duplicate seq
Worker paused (GC, VM freeze) then resumes after losing its leaseIts next append fails with Fenced; it stopsNo event from the stale owner is committed
Tool call succeeds, worker dies before recording the resultNew owner re-issues the call with the same idempotency keyTool sees one logical call; result recorded once
Pub/Sub redelivers an event 5 timesInbox rejects duplicatesAlert sent once, graph merge unchanged
Partition between engine and PostgresAppends fail; runs pause, nothing is lostNo partial state; resumes on heal
Redis unavailableLimiters fall back to local budgets with reduced rate; fail closed for per-target active toolsPer-target rate never exceeds the configured ceiling
Scope store unreachableCached scope until TTL, then denyNo out-of-scope request leaves the egress proxy
Clock skew of 60 seconds on one workerNo effect on ownership (database clock); log timestamps flaggedNo double ownership
Duplicate graph observations, any orderSame graph stateConvergence property (23.5)
Raft leader isolated (implementation B)New leader elected; old leader's commands rejected after it steps downNo two committed values at one index
  • Fault-injection harness: a test mode where every store, network and tool call passes through an adapter that can drop, delay, duplicate, reorder or fail it, driven by a seeded random generator. After each scenario run the invariant checker over the event logs: per-run seq contiguity, one writer per fence, idempotent effects, scope never violated, sagas complete or compensated.
  • Linearizability check: record client-visible operation histories against the event store and verify them with a Wing and Gong style checker (or an existing tool such as Porcupine in Go, Knossos or Elle for the idea).
  • Deterministic simulation testing (stretch): because the VM is already deterministic, run the whole cluster in one process with a simulated clock, simulated network and a seeded scheduler. Any failing seed reproduces exactly. This is the style used by FoundationDB and TigerBeetle; in Rust it pushes the design toward abstracting time, I/O and randomness behind traits from the start.
  • Chaos in the cloud: kill Cloud Run instances, force Postgres failover, inject latency with the egress proxy, and re-run the invariant checker on the resulting logs.

23.9 Phases

PhaseDeliverableLearning focus
D0Single process, Postgres EventStore, lease plus fence, idempotent effects, outbox/inboxCorrect single-writer under crashes
D1Multiple workers, rendezvous placement, heartbeats, back-pressure, fault-injection harness, invariant checkerSafety versus efficiency, testing distributed code
D2Scatter-gather search, hedged requests, convergent graph merge property tests, leased rate budgetsTail latency, CRDT thinking
D3Raft library, single group on a simulated network, then multi-Raft EventStore on GKE StatefulSetsConsensus, snapshots, membership
D4Deterministic simulation of the whole cluster, Pregel-style partitioned analyticsReproducible failure, bulk-synchronous computation

24. Modular architecture and module contracts

The system is built as a set of modules that can be implemented, tested and benchmarked one at a time, in isolation, in any order that respects the dependency rules. The style is clean (hexagonal, ports and adapters): a pure core with no I/O, surrounded by traits (ports) whose real implementations (adapters) live at the edge. A module is "done" only when it satisfies the definition in 24.6, which includes its tests and benchmarks.

24.1 Principles

  • Pure core, effects at the edge. Domain logic (scope decision, DSL, VM, merge, limiters, CSR algorithms, distance kernels) takes values and returns values. It does not read the clock, generate randomness, touch the network or the disk, or log to a global.
  • Ports only at real boundaries. A trait exists where a second implementation or a fake is needed (24.4). Do not wrap every struct in a trait.
  • Every module ships with an in-memory fake of each port it exposes, so its consumers can be tested without Postgres, Redis or the network.
  • Dependencies point inward and downward (24.3). No cycles. Enforced by a test that parses the package metadata (25.2, test arch/deps).
  • Determinism. Given the same inputs and the same seed, a module produces the same outputs. Time and randomness come from ports.
  • Services are thin. A service binary wires adapters to modules (gRPC handlers, config, telemetry). It contains no domain logic and has few tests of its own beyond wiring.

24.2 Workspace layout

flowforge/
  Cargo.toml                 (workspace)  | for a Go reference: go.work and one module per directory below
  modules.toml               machine-readable module manifest (25.10)

  crates/

Rule: a module may depend only on modules in a lower layer, plus ff-testkit as a dev-dependency. Layer 5 and 6 are leaves. Adapters for Postgres, Redis, GCS and Pub/Sub live behind cargo features (or separate adapter crates) so the pure build has no heavy dependencies and compiles and tests fast.

24.4 Ports (traits at boundaries)

// Specification-level; names are normative, signatures show intent.
pub trait Clock: Send + Sync {
    fn now_wall(&self) -> Timestamp;               // display and staleness only
    fn now_mono(&self) -> MonoInstant;             // monotonic, for deadlines and limiters
}
pub trait Rng: Send { fn next_u64(&mut self) -> u64; }

pub trait ToolExec: Send + Sync {                   // engine -> tool-runner
    async fn execute(&self, call: ToolCall, ctx: CallCtx) -> Result<ToolResult, ToolError>;
}
pub trait ScopeCheck: Send + Sync {                 // engine -> scope service
    async fn check(&self, t: Target, class: ActivityClass, at: Timestamp) -> Decision;
}
pub trait Llm: Send + Sync {                        // agent -> model provider
    async fn complete(&self, req: LlmRequest) -> Result<LlmResponse, LlmError>;
}
pub trait Embedder: Send + Sync {
    async fn embed(&self, text: &str, model: &ModelId) -> Result<Vec<f32>, EmbedError>;
}
pub trait VectorIndex: Send + Sync {                // section 22
    fn upsert(&self, id: EntityId, v: &[f32], seq: u64) -> Result<(), IndexError>;
    fn delete(&self, id: EntityId, seq: u64) -> Result<(), IndexError>;
    fn search(&self, q: &[f32], p: &SearchParams) -> Result<Vec<Hit>, IndexError>;
    fn info(&self) -> IndexInfo;
}
pub trait EventStore { /* section 23.4 */ }
pub trait Placement: Send + Sync {
    fn owner(&self, key: &[u8], live: &[WorkerId]) -> Option<WorkerId>;
    fn ranking(&self, key: &[u8], live: &[WorkerId]) -> Vec<WorkerId>;   // preference order for claiming
}
pub trait Transport { /* Raft: send(peer, msg), recv() */ }

24.5 Isolation rules for implementers

  • A module's tests MUST pass with only its own dependencies built (cargo test -p ff-scope never compiles Postgres drivers or tonic).
  • Conformance data is read from conformance/<suite>/; a module never embeds expected values that also exist as data.
  • No module reads environment variables, the system clock, or global state. Configuration arrives as a struct.
  • Public API changes in a lower layer require updating the module's CHANGELOG entry and its conformance data version.
  • Error types are per module (thiserror in Rust, sentinel errors in Go) and mapped to gRPC and REST codes only in services (section 11.8).

24.6 Definition of done for a module

An agent or a human may mark a module complete only when all of the following are true. cargo xtask verify-module <name> checks the mechanical items and fails otherwise.

  1. The public surface in 24.3 exists, is documented (/// on every public item), and has no unwrap() or expect() outside tests (Clippy lint unwrap_used denied).
  2. All required test cases for the module in 25.3 exist as tests whose names start with the case ids (for example scope_001_trailing_dot), and pass.
  3. All conformance suites listed for the module in modules.toml exist under conformance/ and pass.
  4. All property tests in 25.4 for the module exist and pass with 1,000 cases locally and 100,000 in the nightly job.
  5. Fuzz targets in 25.5 exist and run for 60 seconds without findings (nightly: 30 minutes).
  6. The benchmarks in 25.8 for the module exist, run with cargo bench -p <name>, and a baseline result file is committed under bench/results/<module>/.
  7. No forbidden dependency edge (arch/deps test passes); cargo clippy -- -D warnings and cargo fmt --check are clean.
  8. Any unsafe block is covered by a Miri test and carries a // SAFETY: comment stating the invariant.
  9. A short README.md in the module: purpose, the section of this spec it implements, how to run its tests and benchmarks, and known limitations.

24.7 Suggested implementation order (each step needs only completed steps)

StepModulesWhy this orderWhat you learn

1ff-limiterSmall, pure, rich in concurrency and atomicsAtomics, memory ordering, loom, benchmarking
2ff-scopePure, security critical, test-heavyParsing, normalization, exhaustive edge cases
3ff-oamDomain types and the convergent mergeType modelling, property testing
4ff-csrPure algorithms over arraysData layout, iterative algorithms, mmap, unsafe with Miri
5ff-dsl, ff-bytecode, ff-vmFront end, then format, then interpreterParsing, type checking, VM design, fuzzing
6ff-eventstore (mem, then Postgres), ff-engineNeeds VM and storeAsync Rust, sqlx, leases, fencing, replay
7ff-placement, ff-simMulti-worker and fault injection over the engineDistributed correctness testing
8Services, gRPC, auth, alerts, gateway, TerraformWire the modules; deploy to GCPtonic, axum, GCP, API design
9ff-vecindex (pgvector, then DiskANN)Independent of the engineANN algorithms, SSD layout, recall/QPS trade-offs
10ff-agentNeeds DSL, engine, scopeAgent safety design, eval harness
11ff-raft (optional)Replaces the Postgres store behind the same traitConsensus, snapshots, membership

25. Test and benchmark plan

This section is a contract with whoever writes the code (including a coding agent): the tests and benchmarks below are part of the deliverable, written together with the module, not afterwards. A failing or missing item blocks the module's definition of done (24.6).

25.1 Test layers

LayerTool (Rust)ScopeRuns
Unit#[test]One function or type; no I/OEvery commit
ConformanceData-driven test reading conformance/Behaviour specified in this documentEvery commit
PropertyproptestLaws that hold for all inputs (25.4)1,000 cases per commit, 100,000 nightly
DifferentialTest comparing two implementationsFast versus reference (25.6)Every commit (small), nightly (large)
Fuzzcargo-fuzz (libFuzzer)Parsers and decoders: no panic, no hang, no UB (25.5)60 s per target per PR, 30 min nightly
Concurrency modelloomAtomics and lock-free code, small thread countsEvery commit for modules that use atomics
Memory model / UBmiriModules with unsafeNightly and on unsafe changes
Integrationtestcontainers (Postgres, Redis, Pub/Sub emulator)Adapters and services against real dependenciesEvery PR
Simulationff-simWhole system under seeded faults, invariant check (25.7)200 seeds per PR, 10,000 nightly
SnapshotinstaDiagnostics text, generated code, JSON shapesEvery commit
End to endScript against a deployed dev environmentSignup, create program, run workflow, approve, alertPost-deploy

25.2 Rules for all tests

  • Deterministic. No wall-clock sleeps (use FakeClock), no real network in unit tests, no shared mutable global. A randomised test prints its seed on failure and accepts the seed through an environment variable (FF_SEED) to reproduce.
  • Named by id. Required cases in 25.3 have stable ids; the test function starts with the id. xtask verify-module greps for them.
  • Architecture test arch/deps. Parses cargo metadata, builds the module graph and asserts: layer rule from 24.3, no cycles, no adapter crate in a pure module's dependency tree, no std::time::SystemTime::now or Instant::now or rand::thread_rng usage in layer 0 to 4 crates (a simple source scan plus a Clippy disallowed_methods configuration).
  • Every bug gets a regression test named regress_<issue>, ideally a conformance case.
  • Coverage is a signal, not a target: report line and branch coverage (cargo llvm-cov); pure modules are expected to stay high, and a drop below the previous baseline needs an explanation in the PR. For ff-scope and ff-limiter also run mutation testing (cargo-mutants) nightly; surviving mutants in decision logic are treated as missing tests.
  • No flaky tests. A test that fails intermittently is quarantined within a day and fixed or deleted within a week.

25.4 Property-based tests

ModuleProperty
ff-scopenormalize(normalize(x)) == normalize(x); check never panics for any string; adding a deny rule never turns a deny into an allow; removing an allow rule never turns a deny into an allow
ff-oamConvergence: for any list of observations, any permutation and any duplication yield an identical graph state. Merge is commutative, associative and idempotent.
ff-limiterFor any timeline of requests, total allowed in any window [t, t + w] is at most burst + rate * w. All variants agree with a simple reference model (a list of timestamps) on generated timelines.
ff-csrBuild from a random edge list: degree sums equal E; each row sorted; transposed of transposed equals the original; BFS equals a naive adjacency-list BFS; Tarjan SCC equals a naive reachability-based SCC; PageRank equals a dense-matrix power iteration; snapshot plus overlay equals rebuild.
ff-dslPretty-print then parse returns the same AST; generated well-typed programs always check; compiled program evaluated by the tree-walker equals the VM result.
ff-bytecode / ff-vmAny module accepted by the verifier never causes a VM panic or stack error for any inputs; encode/decode identity; fuel accounting is monotonic.
ff-eventstoreUnder random interleavings of append, lease and read from several simulated workers, per-run seq is contiguous and each fence value has one writer.
ff-engineCrash at a random point, recover, continue: final event log equals the log of an uninterrupted run (up to timestamps).
ff-vecindexResults are always a subset of live ids; distances are non-decreasing in the result list; after deleting everything, searches are empty; recall@k is monotonic non-decreasing in L on a fixed dataset (within tolerance).
ff-placementOwner is a member of the live set; adding a worker only moves keys to the new worker.
// Illustrative: convergence property for ff-oam (names are not normative)
proptest! {
    #![proptest_config(ProptestConfig::with_cases(1000))]
    #[test]
    fn oam_prop_merge_converges(obs in vec(arb_observation(), 0..60), seed in any::<u64>()) {
        let baseline = Graph::from_observations(&obs);
        let mut shuffled = obs.clone();
        SeededRng::new(seed).shuffle(&mut shuffled);
        let mut with_dups = shuffled.clone();
        with_dups.extend(shuffled.iter().take(10).cloned());
        prop_assert_eq!(baseline.canonical_bytes(), Graph::from_observations(&with_dups).canonical_bytes());
    }
}

25.5 Fuzz targets

Invariants for every target: no panic, no undefined behaviour (run with sanitizers), no allocation beyond a limit, finishes within a time limit. A crashing input is committed to fuzz/corpus/regress/ and becomes a unit test.

TargetInputExtra check
fuzz_lexer, fuzz_parserArbitrary bytes as sourceOn success, pretty-print and re-parse gives the same AST
fuzz_checkerStructured (grammar-aware) programsDiagnostics have spans inside the source
fuzz_bytecode_decode, fuzz_verifierArbitrary bytes as a moduleIf verified, running it with small fuel never panics
fuzz_snapshot_decodeArbitrary bytes as a CBOR snapshotRejected or loads and runs safely
fuzz_scope_normalizeArbitrary strings as targetIdempotent; never matches a rule it should not (compare against a slow reference matcher)
fuzz_jwtArbitrary token stringsNever accepted without a valid signature
fuzz_rest_jsonArbitrary request bodies per endpointAlways a defined 4xx, never a 5xx
fuzz_csr_open, fuzz_segment_openArbitrary bytes as fileClean error; no out-of-bounds read (also under Miri on small inputs)
fuzz_alert_ruleArbitrary rule textDiagnostics or a rule; never a panic

25.6 Differential tests

  • Tree-walking evaluator versus bytecode VM (every vm/run case plus generated programs).
  • Naive reference versus optimised implementation: CSR algorithms versus adjacency-list versions; GCRA/atomic version versus the timestamp-list model; SIMD versus scalar kernels; rendezvous versus a slow recomputation.
  • Exact versus approximate: BruteForceIndex versus DiskANN and versus pgvector (recall, not equality).
  • Implementation versus implementation: MemStore versus Postgres versus Raft behind the EventStore conformance suite.
  • Language versus language: Go reference and Rust port run the same conformance/ data and must produce identical outputs (section 20.2).

25.7 Simulation and invariants

ff-sim runs the engine, several workers, the event store, a fake tool executor, a fake scope service and a fake Pub/Sub inside one process, with every call going through an adapter that can drop, delay, duplicate, reorder or fail it, and with a simulated clock. A scenario is (seed, fault_profile, workload). After the run, check_invariants(history) must return no violations:

IdInvariant
inv_01Per run, event seq is contiguous from 1 with no duplicates.
inv_02Each fence value has at most one writer, and fences strictly increase over time.
inv_03No event is committed by a writer whose fence was already superseded.
inv_04Every tool effect has at most one recorded result; the tool executor may have run it more than once only with the same idempotency key.
inv_05Replaying a run's log reproduces its terminal state and its effect order.
inv_06No active tool call was issued for a target without a prior allow decision in the same run for that target and scope version.
inv_07Every saga that failed has either completed compensation in reverse order or is marked as needing operator action.
inv_08Every alert-worthy event produced exactly one delivered alert per rule and dedupe window (duplicates in the transport do not produce duplicate alerts).
inv_09The graph state after the simulation equals the state obtained by applying the set of recorded observations once each in a canonical order.
inv_10Per-target request rate observed by the fake tool executor never exceeds the configured ceiling over any window.
inv_11Every submitted run reaches a terminal state, or is still non-terminal only because the workload ended (liveness within a bounded number of simulated seconds after faults stop).

Fault profiles: none, crashy (workers killed at random instants), lossy (drop and duplicate messages), slow (random latency spikes), partitioned (workers cut from the store for intervals), skewed (clock offsets), and a chaos mix. CI runs 200 seeds per profile per PR and 10,000 nightly; a failing seed is printed and added to conformance/sim/regress_seeds.json.

25.8 Benchmarks

Methodology (applies to all).

  • Micro benchmarks use criterion (statistical, with warm-up and outlier reporting); where CI noise is a problem also record instruction counts (iai-callgrind or perf stat) because they are stable across machines.
  • Macro benchmarks live in bench/ and use a purpose-built load generator (open-loop with a target rate, to avoid coordinated omission). They report throughput and latency percentiles p50, p90, p99, p99.9 from an HDR histogram.
  • Fixed seeds and generated datasets (xtask gen-bench-data) so any two runs use identical inputs. External datasets (SIFT1M, GIST1M) are downloaded by script, never committed.
  • Record the environment in every result: CPU model, cores, RAM, OS, kernel, rustc version, build profile (--release with lto = "thin", codegen-units = 1), commit, and whether the CPU governor was pinned.
  • Results format: one JSON file per run under bench/results/<module>/<date>-<commit>.json with {bench_id, params, metrics: {name: value, unit}, env}; xtask bench-compare <baseline> <new> prints deltas.
  • Targets are measured, then fixed. The first run of each benchmark produces the baseline. Acceptance thresholds below are expressed as shapes and ratios (scaling, speed-ups, recall floors) that can be checked without guessing absolute numbers; absolute budgets are set after the baseline and recorded in bench/budgets.toml.
  • Regression gate: nightly compares against the stored baseline; a regression of more than 10% in a stable metric (instruction counts) or 20% in a timing metric, sustained over three runs, opens an issue. A PR that deliberately slows something updates the baseline with a stated reason.
  • Every benchmark also tracks allocations per operation (dhat or a counting allocator) because accidental allocation in hot paths is the most common regression.
IdModuleWhat it measuresWorkload and parametersMetricsAcceptance shape

Required write-up per benchmark family (in bench/REPORT.md): the question asked, the setup, a chart or table, one paragraph of interpretation (what limited the result), and one flamegraph (cargo flamegraph or perf) for the slowest path. This is the artefact to show in an interview.

conformance/

// conformance/limiter/gcra/basic.json  (the section 14.5 vector)
{
  "id": "lim_001",
  "config": { "rate_per_s": 10, "burst": 5 },
  "steps": [
    { "t_ms": 0,   "cost": 1, "expect": { "allow": true } },
    { "t_ms": 0,   "cost": 1, "expect": { "allow": true } },
    { "t_ms": 0,   "cost": 1, "expect": { "allow": true } },
    { "t_ms": 0,   "cost": 1, "expect": { "allow": true } },
    { "t_ms": 0,   "cost": 1, "expect": { "allow": true } },
    { "t_ms": 0,   "cost": 1, "expect": { "allow": false, "retry_after_ms": 100 } },
    { "t_ms": 100, "cost": 1, "expect": { "allow": true } }
  ]
}
// Illustrative data-driven runner (names are not normative)
#[test]
fn lim_001_gcra_conformance() {
    for case in testkit::load_cases::<GcraCase>("limiter/gcra") {
        let clock = FakeClock::new();
        let limiter = Gcra::new(case.config.rate_per_s, case.config.burst, clock.clone());
        for (i, step) in case.steps.iter().enumerate() {
            clock.set_mono_ms(step.t_ms);
            let got = limiter.check("k", step.cost);
            assert_eq!(got.allow, step.expect.allow, "{} step {}", case.id, i);
            if let Some(ms) = step.expect.retry_after_ms {
                assert_eq!(got.retry_after.as_millis() as u64, ms, "{} step {}", case.id, i);
            }
        }
    }
}

25.10 Module manifest and the verify command

modules.toml is the machine-readable form of sections 24 and 25. xtask verify-module reads it, so a coding agent can check its own work and a human can see what is missing.

CommandDoes
cargo xtask verify-module <name>Checks the 24.6 list: required test ids exist (name scan) and pass, conformance directories exist and are non-empty, property, fuzz, loom and bench targets exist, baseline result file committed, arch/deps passes, Clippy and fmt clean, README present. Prints a table of missing items and exits non-zero if any.
cargo xtask test-module <name>Runs unit, conformance and property tests of one module (and its loom and Miri tests when listed).
cargo xtask bench-module <name>Runs the module's benchmarks and writes result JSON.
cargo xtask bench-compare <a> <b>Compares two result files and prints per-metric deltas.
cargo xtask fuzz-smokeRuns every fuzz target for 60 seconds.
cargo xtask sim --profile chaos --seeds 200Runs the simulation and checks the invariants in 25.7.
cargo xtask gen-conformanceRegenerates derived conformance data (for example reference PageRank) from the reference implementation; the diff must be reviewed by hand.

25.11 CI stages

  1. Fast (every push): fmt, clippy -D warnings, cargo deny (licences, advisories), arch/deps, unit and conformance tests, property tests at 1,000 cases, loom tests, snapshot tests.
  2. Integration (every PR): Postgres, Redis and the Pub/Sub emulator through testcontainers; adapter and service tests; EventStore suite against Postgres; 200 simulation seeds; 60-second fuzz smoke.
  3. Nightly: property tests at 100,000 cases, 30-minute fuzz per target, Miri on unsafe modules, sanitizers (address, thread), mutation testing on ff-scope and ff-limiter, 10,000 simulation seeds across all fault profiles, full benchmark suite with comparison to the baseline, coverage report.
  4. Post-deploy to dev: end-to-end script, the system benchmarks b_sys_*, and a chaos run that kills workers and forces a Postgres failover, followed by the invariant checker over the exported logs.

25.12 Guidance for a coding agent

  • Work on one module at a time in the order of 24.7. Start by reading the module's section, its row in 24.3, its test table in 25.3 and its modules.toml entry.
  • Write the test list and the conformance data first (they will fail), then implement until they pass. Do not edit expected values to make a test pass without a written justification that cites this specification.
  • When this specification is ambiguous or inconsistent (for example fields flagged verify), do not guess silently: add a docs/open-questions.md entry in the module, pick the most conservative behaviour (fail closed for anything touching scope), and add a test that pins the choice.
  • Finish by running cargo xtask verify-module <name> and cargo xtask bench-module <name>, commit the baseline result, and summarise: what was implemented, what the benchmark showed, what remains.
  • Never weaken a safety invariant (scope, fencing, idempotency, determinism) to improve a benchmark.

Part II. Flowforge v2: content capture, change diffs and diff search

Part II specifies what v2 adds to Part I: workflows that capture what targets serve (HTML, JavaScript, CSS, XML, JSON, headers, certificates), store every version compactly, compute noise-robust diffs, turn meaningful changes into graph changes and alerts, and make the whole change history searchable. Every parser is an explicit state machine of our own, so the Rust port is a translation of the same machines checked against the same data.

Version 0.4 (draft)Date 2026-11-13Sources: docs/V2_DESIGN.md (the brief), the v2 package READMEs, FORMAT.md files and OPEN_QUESTIONS.md files; the code is the reference where they differ (section 41)

26. Part II overview and portability rules

26.1 How Part II relates to Part I and to the brief

Part II uses the conventions of 1.2 and the module rules of 24 and 25 unchanged. Sections 26 to 42 continue the numbering of Part I; section 42 corrects Part I where its text did not describe the code. They replace docs/V2_DESIGN.md as the normative text: the brief stays as the design record, and where it and the code disagree the code was followed and the difference is listed in section 41. Byte formats are given byte by byte; where a package ships a FORMAT.md (chunkstore, textindex) that file is the same contract in more detail and MUST agree with this text.

26.2 Status of v2

Built and passing verify-module: every package in sections 28 to 34, the egress features of 35, the tools of 36, the REST surface of 37 and the lab acceptance of 38. Carried to v3 (not part of this specification's MUSTs yet): the A1 sandbox runtime, supply-chain signing of external binaries, the Raft and distributed track (B), the performance track (C), headless rendering (D6), the catalog change class (needs the advisory matcher, decision D4), a learned ranker. Re-derive jobs after a normalizer or parser upgrade (33.10, G-CST-04) are specified here and were being implemented on feat_v2 as this part was written. The Cloud Storage backends (chunkstore/gcsstore: a chunkstore.Backend and a textindex.Directory) and the content service's Terraform were being added on feat_v2 as this part was written (G-CST-01, G-CST-02); they implement the existing ports and change no format.

26.3 Portability rules (v2 packages MUST follow them; v1 packages SHOULD)

These are in addition to docs/CONVENTIONS.md.

  1. State machines are explicit. A tokenizer is a state enum plus a loop that switches on it. State names follow the governing specification (WHATWG "tag open state" is stateTagOpen) and each state cites its section. No regular expressions in tokenizers, no parser generators.
  2. Bytes in, spans out. Parsers take []byte (UTF-8 after decoding), report positions as byte offsets (plus line and column where users see them), and never retain the input after returning unless documented.
  3. Trees are arenas. DOM and AST nodes live in one slice and refer to each other by int32 index (NodeID, -1 = none), never by pointer. This maps 1:1 to a Rust Vec<Node>.
  4. No any, interface{} or reflection in core data paths. No map iteration order may reach an output: sort, or use insertion-ordered structures.
  5. No goroutines in pure packages unless the parallelism is optional, deterministic and off by default.
  6. Binary formats are specified byte by byte: little-endian, explicit lengths, versioned magic, CRC32C (Castagnoli, crc32c("123456789") = 0xE3069283), and a CRC or SHA-256 footer. No Go-specific encodings. Durations are integer milliseconds or nanoseconds; times are RFC 3339 UTC with milliseconds or Unix nanoseconds.
  7. Compression is zstd (RFC 8878), github.com/klauspost/compress/zstd in Go and the zstd crate in Rust, with levels and windows pinned per format. Formats never depend on compressed bytes: identity is a hash of raw data and integrity is re-checked after decoding (31.2).
  8. Errors are stable codes. Each parser has a closed list of codes returned as values (section 40); recovery behaviour is part of the contract.
  9. Determinism. The same input gives the same bytes: trees, diffs, chunk boundaries, index segments, hashes. Every seeded table is generated from core.SplitMix64 with a documented seed (31.1, 30.4).
  10. Tests are invariants. Every package ships conformance data in conformance/<suite>/, numbered invariants <PKG>-INV-NN each with a test Test<Pkg>Inv<NN>_<Name> (section 39), differential tests against an oracle where one exists, fuzz targets asserting the invariants, and benchmarks with an acceptance shape. A test that only checks a happy path returns something is not acceptable.

26.4 Reading grades and amplification bounds

Every reader reports a grade: complete (read everything), partial (a bound was hit or the reader recovered), unsupported (a form it refuses by design, for example a DTD), or failed (could not read). "Read fine, found nothing" (complete, no facts) is never confused with "could not read" (failed). Unreadable input is counted and audited, never fatal. A grade below complete never withdraws graph facts (28.4).

The bounds below are identical in every implementation. A document that hits one is read partially (grade partial) and the bound is reported with its code.

BoundValueWhereCode when hit
Response body read8 MiB (8 << 20)content.MaxResponseBytes, collect, every parser's input capinput_truncated / input_too_large
HTML open-element depth512htmlparse.DefaultMaxDepth, content.MaxHTMLDepthnesting-depth-limit-exceeded (element inserted childless, parsing continues)
HTML attributes per tag1024htmlparse.DefaultMaxAttributesattribute-limit-exceeded
HTML input (library default)256 MiB; content passes 8 MiBhtmlparse.DefaultMaxInputSizeinput-size-limit-exceeded
JavaScript syntactic nesting4000 (statements, expressions, binary-operator chains; each member, call and tagged-template subscript of a chain counts one level)jsparse.DefaultMaxDepth; content uses the defaulttoo-deep
XML and CSS nesting64content.MaxParseDepth, xmlparse, cssparse MaxDepthdepth_limit
Facts per extractor per document20,000content.MaxFactsPerParser; also cssparse.MaxURLs, sourcemap.MaxSources, wellknown itemslimit_exceeded
Inner documents per capture200 (inline scripts, styles, handlers, source-map originals)content.MaxInnerDocumentsgrade partial
Decompressionmin(MaxOutput, max(RatioFloor, MaxRatio × len(src))); defaults 8 MiB, 100×, 1 MiBcodec.Inflateoutput_limit, ratio_limit
Recursive decodingdepth 3, 8 MiB total, 256 nodescodec.DecodeLayersbounded, never an error
robots.txt parsed500 KiB (cut at a line boundary), 20,000 ruleswellknowninput_too_large, limit_exceeded
Sitemap20,000 entries / 8 MiB by default, raisable to 50,000 / 50 MiBxmlparse/sitemapgrade partial
Diff10,000 token edits; 4 MiB normalized at token level; 64 MiB at line leveldiff.BoundedDiffrecorded fallback (30.2)

26.5 Determinism points

These outputs are compared byte for byte across implementations (or, where noted, after decompression):

  • Chunk boundaries and chunk ids (31.1), manifest bytes and ManifestIDs (31.5); pack, index and ref bytes only for the same compressor (31.8).
  • Text-index segments except the zstd data area of STORED, which is compared decompressed (32.6).
  • Normalized text and its rule-table SHA-256 (33.3); feature sets, change sets, scores and observations (CONTENT-INV-12).
  • Edit scripts, unified text, side-by-side JSON, JSON patches and tree mappings (DIFF-INV-13).
  • SimHash, MinHash and LSH outputs (30.4).
  • Derived ids: graph ids (Part I, PORTING_RUST 4.1) and content-store ids (34.3).
  • BM25 scores are compared at 1e-9 relative: ln is not correctly rounded in either language (32.4).

27. v2 module catalogue

The layering rule of 24.3 holds. Layer-1 packages import only core (and the standard library); the extra dependencies allowed per package are listed. modules.toml carries one entry per package with its required invariant ids, conformance suites, fuzz targets and benchmarks; go run ./cmd/xtask verify-module <name> checks them.

xmlparse/sitemap lives under xmlparse because layer-1 packages may not import each other: wellknown therefore does not parse sitemaps, the content pipeline calls both. vecindex was refactored to use sketch with no change of behaviour; its conformance data still passes (SKETCH-INV-01).

Implementation order (extends 24.7): the layer-1 parsers, codec, diff, sketch, chunkstore and textindex need only core and can be built in any order right after it; then the oam and graphstore v2 parts, content, contentstore, the egress features, the tools and service, and the lab last.

28. Graph expansion

All additions are Flowforge extensions ff. OAM import and export keep working because unknown types are stored opaquely (5.2).

28.1 Endpoint asset

AssetContent fieldsCanonical key
Endpointmethod (upper case, or *), url_template, source (html_form | js_literal | js_call | openapi | graphql | sitemap | observed)ep:<METHOD> <url_template>

source is not part of the key and merges last-writer-wins. The method is upper-cased although RFC 9110 methods are case-sensitive. NormalizeURLTemplate MUST apply these steps, and its result is a fixed point:

  1. Trim Unicode white space; reject an empty string, one longer than 4,096 bytes (MaxURLTemplateLen), and any ASCII control character (U+0000 to U+001F, U+007F) left after trimming.
  2. Recognize placeholders left to right: a JS template substitution ${...} (braces nest; unterminated is an error) becomes {expr}; {name} with name matching [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_.-]* is kept as {id}, {uuid}, {hex} or {expr} when it is one of those and becomes {expr} otherwise. Other braces are literal text; percent-encoded braces are never placeholders.
  3. Scheme and host are concrete: the text before the first placeholder contains scheme://authority followed by /, ?, # or the end. Hosts are never templated. Relative and protocol-relative templates are errors (the caller resolves them against the page origin first). Only http and https.
  4. Query and fragment are dropped (query parameters are ParameterProperty tags).
  5. A path segment containing a placeholder becomes that placeholder when it is exactly one, and {expr} otherwise.
  6. The concrete URL (placeholder segments replaced by sentinels) is normalized by the 4.2 URL rules; .. after a placeholder removes it.
  7. Every concrete segment of ASCII digits becomes {id}, one shaped 8-4-4-4-12 hex digits {uuid}, one of at least 16 hex digits (MinHexSegment) {hex}, compared after step 6 (so %31%32 is numeric).

Empty segments and a trailing slash are kept; the root path is /. Conformance: conformance/oam/url_template/normalize.json, conformance/oam/keys/endpoint.json.

28.2 Relation labels

All are SimpleRelation. Each label belongs to a rule family, the unit of coverage (28.4). They are validated like the 5.5 docs labels (endpoint types enforced).

LabelFrom → ToFamilyMeaning
loads_scriptURL → Filescripts<script src>, module imports, import()
loads_styleURL or File → Filestyles<link rel=stylesheet>, CSS @import
embedsURL → URLframesiframe, object, embed
links_toURL → URLlinksanchors, <link> other than stylesheet, robots and sitemap links
redirects_toURL → URLredirectsobserved redirect hop; optional content status (300-399), not part of the identity, rejected on any other label
form_actionURL → Endpointformsform submission target
callsFile or URL → EndpointendpointsAPI call found in JS or observed
source_mapFile → Filesourcemapsbundle → its source map
previous_versionFile → Filelineagechunk lineage across renamed bundles
includes_libraryFile → ProductReleaselibrarieslibrary banner or fingerprint inside a bundle
documentsURL → EndpointschemasOpenAPI or GraphQL schema describing an endpoint

Tag families: headers (HTTPHeaderProperty), content (ContentProperty), parameters (ParameterProperty), findings (VulnProperty). Unknown labels and property types are their own family. The registration label additionally joins FQDN → DomainRecord and AutonomousSystem → AutnumRecord (an extension of the 5.5 row used by the rdap and asn tools).

PropertyFieldsTag nameRules
ContentPropertysnapshot_id, body_sha256, size, mime, profile, normalizer_version, last_changed_at, change_countthe profileOne per (entity, profile): the graph's pointer into the content store. last_changed_at RFC 3339 UTC with milliseconds; size and change_count always encoded
HTTPHeaderPropertyname, valuethe header nameSecurity-relevant headers only (CSP, HSTS, CORS, X-Frame-Options, Server, cookie attribute summaries). authorization, proxy-authorization and cookie are rejected, and so is a set-cookie value with any k=v whose key is not a cookie attribute

VulnProperty with category = "exposed_secret"id secret:<rule>:<hmac hex>, enum = rule id, desc = redacted previewthe idHolds only the rule id, the preview (first 4 and last 2 characters, xxxx...yy; ... for secrets under 12 bytes or with non-printable bytes) and a keyed hash (HMAC-SHA256 under the tenant's hash data key, 34.4). The secret itself never enters the graph or logs (OAM-INV-11)

28.4 Authority and coverage-gated staleness

An observation MAY carry authority = {source, profile, coverage, covered_rules} with coverage one of complete, partial, failed, unsupported. The rules (oam OPEN_QUESTIONS 22-29 are the full text):

  1. Units. A unit is (source entity, profile, rule family). Edges leaving authority.source and non-source tags on it are authoritative: they report to the unit of their family and do not register the tool for covering runs. Everything else in the observation stays classic (5.7).
  2. Gate. A reading replaces a unit's member set only when coverage = complete and the family is in covered_rules; a complete reading that reports nothing for a covered family empties that unit. Every other reading (partial, failed, unsupported, or families outside covered_rules) only adds members. Withdrawal is not retraction: the record goes stale with history kept (edge_removed, tag hidden) and returns when re-reported.
  3. Convergence. Readings are ordered by (observed_at, tool, observation id). A unit's effective members are the members of the latest complete reading plus those reported by any newer non-complete reading; the state is a function of the multiset of observations (OAM-INV-05).
  4. Liveness. A record is active iff it has classic support that covering runs have not made stale, or authoritative support (an edge held by at least one unit, an entity that is the target of such an edge). Covering runs never stale a record with authoritative support; a withdrawal stales at once only records without other support. Records that only an older complete capture reports are created and stale in the same operation.
  5. When a capture is complete is decided by content (33.8): the capture outcome is complete (no truncation, no collection-integrity flag), the body normalized completely, every extractor that ran graded complete and none applicable is canary-disabled (CONTENT-INV-14). Coverage is decided per capture, before any diff, so a diff fallback does not affect it (section 41).
  6. The covering-run staleness of 5.7 is unchanged for every other observation.

Event order within one observation (event ids derive from the emission index, so the order is normative): entity events in observation order, edge events, then liveness transitions caused by authority (edges by id, then entities by id), then tag_changed (entity tags before edge tags, by target id, type, name).

28.5 Change events: tag_changed, content_changed, base, cause, baseline

  • tag_changed: one event per tag whose visible content changed per operation, for every property type except SourceProperty. Patch: [{"op":"add","path":"","value":{...}}] when the tag appears, [{"op":"remove","path":""}] when it disappears (a root remove, which RFC 6902 cannot express), field-level add, replace and remove ops otherwise. The event carries tag_type and tag_name (OAM-INV-07).
  • Base. entity_added carries base = {content, scope_status}; the edge_added that creates an edge carries base = {content}; reactivations carry none. Edge content changes still emit no event, so a replay reconstructs edge existence, state and tags but not later edge content.
  • Cause and baseline. Observation.cause (target by default, parser, normalizer) and baseline are copied onto every event the observation produces; target is stored as NULL; covering-run events carry neither (OAM-INV-10).
  • content_changed is appended by RecordContentChange(entity, run, at, patch). It changes no state and is idempotent per (entity, snapshot_from, snapshot_to, diff_id): an identical repeat returns the same event (same id and seq) and appends nothing (OAM-INV-09).
// content_changed patch (oam.ContentChangePatch), after Normalize
{
  "snapshot_from": "snp_...",          // optional; absent for a baseline
  "snapshot_to": "snp_...",            // required; != snapshot_from; ids at most 256 printable bytes
  "diff_id": "cdf_...",                // optional: the content store diff id
  "levels": ["hash", "text", "semantic"],   // at least one; canonical order, duplicates removed
  "summary": { "endpoints_added": 1 }, // lower-case identifier keys, counts >= 0
  "cause": "target",                   // target | parser | normalizer (empty means target)
  "baseline": false,
  "class": "semantic_endpoint",        // structural | metadata | semantic_endpoint | observation | catalog | ""
  "score": 0.5,                        // finite, in [0, 1]
  "score_features": [ { "name": "new_endpoint", "weight": 0.25 } ]   // unique lower-case names, sorted, weights in [-1, 1]
}

Migration 0030_graph_v2.sql (idempotent):

ALTER TABLE graph.change_events ADD CONSTRAINT change_events_type_check CHECK (type IN (
  'entity_added','entity_changed','entity_stale','entity_reactivated','edge_added','edge_removed','scope_status_changed',
  'content_changed','tag_changed'));
ALTER TABLE graph.change_events ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS tag_type text;
ALTER TABLE graph.change_events ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS tag_name text;
ALTER TABLE graph.change_events ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS base jsonb;
ALTER TABLE graph.change_events ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS cause text;          -- NULL = target
ALTER TABLE graph.change_events ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS baseline boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false;
ALTER TABLE graph.change_events ADD CONSTRAINT change_events_tag_check
  CHECK ((type = 'tag_changed') = (tag_type IS NOT NULL AND tag_name IS NOT NULL));
ALTER TABLE graph.change_events ADD CONSTRAINT change_events_cause_check
  CHECK (cause IS NULL OR cause IN ('parser','normalizer'));
CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS change_events_entity_seq ON graph.change_events (entity_id, seq) WHERE entity_id IS NOT NULL;
CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS change_events_edge_seq ON graph.change_events (edge_id, seq) WHERE edge_id IS NOT NULL;

Authority bookkeeping needs no column: it lives in hidden tag rows (ff:live per record and ff:auth per unit on the source entity) next to the v1 ff:meta and ff:run rows (G-CORE-06 tracks moving them to columns).

28.6 Commonness

An entity's holders are the distinct hosts of the active entities pointing at it over active edges. It is common when holders > 50 (CommonThreshold); stale edges and repeated hosts do not count. Pivots and leads ignore common nodes; shared infrastructure never implies ownership. Commonness is a query (Store.Commonness), not stored state (OAM-INV-14). The CSR blast-radius labels gain loads_script, loads_style and embeds.

28.7 AsOf reads

GetEntityAsOf(entity, seq) and NeighborsAsOf(request, seq) evaluate on the graph as it was right after change event seq of the program (seq >= 1; NeighborsAsOf requires since zero). A record is reconstructed by folding its change events up to seq, starting from the empty graph:

  • entity_added creates the entity from base (content and scope status), active; entity_changed applies its add and replace ops; scope_status_changed sets the scope status; entity_stale and entity_reactivated set the state;
  • edge_added with base creates the edge, active; without base it reactivates it; edge_removed makes it stale;
  • tag_changed applies its patch to the named tag (a root add creates it, a root remove deletes it); content_changed changes nothing.

Identity (asset type, key, relation, endpoints) comes from the current row; confidence, times, runs, SourceProperty tags and later edge content are not reconstructed and are left zero. An entity whose entity_added is after seq is NOT_FOUND; a bad seq is INVALID_ARGUMENT; traversals skip stale entities and edges as in 5.9 (OAM-INV-12).

The brief describes AsOf as "replaying change events onto the nearest snapshot". The implementation replays from the empty graph (the nearest snapshot is the empty one in this version); a CSR-snapshot starting point is an optimisation with the same results (section 41).

28.8 Graph store conformance (conformance/graphstore)

Language-neutral store-level cases, run against every store implementation (TestGraph001_* to TestGraph006_* on memory, and on Postgres with memory as the oracle when FF_TEST_PG_DSN is set). The file schema extends conformance/oam/merge (steps with observation, complete_run, error, pending; scope, stale_after_runs, order_dependent; expect.entities, expect.edges, expect.changes compared exactly):

{
  "id": "gs_003",
  "description": "...",
  "stale_after_runs": 1,
  "order_dependent": true,           // required when steps include complete_run or content_change
  "steps": [
    { "observation": { ... } },
    { "complete_run": { "tenant_id": "t", "program_id": "p", "run_id": "r1", "tool": "dnsx", "completed_at": "..." } },
    { "content_change": { "entity": "url:https://acme.com/app", "run_id": "r-mon", "at": "...",
                          "patch": { ... }, "repeat": false, "error": "" } }   // error: invalid_argument | not_found
  ],
  "expect": { "entities": [ ... ], "edges": [ ... ], "changes": [ ... ] },
  "as_of": [
    { "after_step": 5,               // the seq reached after the first 5 steps (seqs are store-local)
      "entities":  [ { "key": "fqdn:b.acme.com", "exists": true, "state": "stale", "scope_status": "", "content": null, "tags": null } ],
      "neighbors": [ { "key": "fqdn:b.acme.com", "direction": "both", "depth": 1,
                       "entities": [ "fqdn:b.acme.com stale" ],
                       "edges": [ ] } ] }   // "<from key> <label> <to key> <state>", compared as sets
  ]
}
